{"operation": "move_to_x" | "move_to_y" | "new_block" | "none", "predicate": [{"field": "<field name>", "operator": "equals" | "contains" | "not_contains" | "regex" | "is_null" | "is_not_null" | "gte" | "lte" | "between", "value": "<text>"}], "reason": "<one sentence>"}

Before committing to a condition, weigh what it will do at scale, not just to the pair shown:
- Group identity (X vs Y) is an arbitrary internal label, not a signal of size or quality — never let "X" or "Y" itself factor into your reasoning.
- Pulling k records out of a bigger group into a smaller one can shrink total comparisons even while reuniting entities — a narrow, well-aimed condition beats a broad one that happens to include the seed pair.
- Every record your condition pulls out of a group is cut off from everything else still in that group, including records it was correctly grouped with — a condition that matches too broadly can do more separating than reuniting.
- Prefer a field/value pattern general enough to plausibly cover the unseen majority of a group, not a fragment that only matches the one example pair verbatim.

Before answering, check your condition against the seed pair and the samples above:
- The value you pick must literally appear in the text of the record(s) you intend to match (check the sample, not just the seed pair) — a value invented from memory of similar records elsewhere routinely matches 0 records.
- Prefer a distinctive, near-complete phrase (e.g. most of a title, a model number, an exact name) over a short generic token. Short or generic values (a common venue, manufacturer, single word) tend to match a large, unpredictable slice of the group instead of just the entity you found, which pulls in unrelated records and inflates comparisons far more than the reunited pair is worth.
- If the field that ties the seed pair together is broad (venue, manufacturer, year) and shared by many unrelated records, do not use it alone — narrow it by combining with a second, more specific field, or skip the edit ("none") rather than move a large chunk on a weak signal.
- A condition that would move most of a large group into a much smaller one, or unite two large chunks, almost always costs more in comparisons than it repairs — check the "how the grouping responds" arithmetic before writing it.
- If you cannot find a value in the sample that is both specific to the shared entity and unlikely to sweep in unrelated records, answer "none" for that step rather than guess.

Iteration 8: Selected program 3 score: 0.31276720239071254
Iteration 8: Proposed new text for message: Two groups of records, group X and group Y, are shown below. One pair of records, one from each group, is known to describe the same real-world entity, yet the two records were placed in different groups.

{STATS}

Known same-entity pair:
{SEED}

Group X sample:
{SAMPLE_X}

Group Y sample:
{SAMPLE_Y}

Decide what change to the grouping would place records that describe the same entity together. State the change as a condition on record fields, not as a list of the records you were shown: the condition is applied to every record of the group, including the many that are not shown here.

Before answering, check your condition against the seed pair and the samples above:
- The value you pick must literally appear in the text of the record(s) you intend to match (check the sample, not just the seed pair) — a value invented from memory of similar records elsewhere routinely matches 0 records.
- The strongest conditions quote most of a distinctive field verbatim from the seed record — a near-complete title, a full model number, a full proper name. These tend to match only the handful of records that are true duplicates of the seed and reliably help.
- Never use a venue, publisher, manufacturer, category, year, or other field shared by many unrelated records as your only condition, even with "equals". These look precise but routinely match a large, unpredictable slice of a group (tens to hundreds of records), and the outcome is inconsistent: sometimes it happens to reunite many pairs, but just as often it drags in unrelated records, inflates comparisons far more than it's worth, or nets negative because it separates records that were already correctly grouped elsewhere. If the only signal you have is a broad field, either combine it with a second, more specific field (e.g. venue + a distinctive title fragment) so the pair together is narrow, or answer "none".
- Watch out for near-duplicate titles/names that differ only in a small detail (an edition number, a page range, a co-author list truncated differently) — a condition built on the shared prefix can match both the intended duplicate and a different-but-similar record, which shows up as pairs pulled apart instead of joined. When the field that ties the seed pair together is shared by other near-variants in the sample, narrow it with a second field (author, pages, year) rather than matching on the common prefix alone.
- A condition that would move most of a large group into a much smaller one, or unite two large chunks, almost always costs more in comparisons than it repairs — check the "how the grouping responds" arithmetic before writing it.
- If you cannot find a value in the sample that is both specific to the shared entity and unlikely to sweep in unrelated records, answer "none" for that step rather than guess. A missed repair costs nothing; a wrong broad match costs comparisons across the whole collection.
- "new_block" is for isolating a suspect subset you're not ready to commit to either existing group — use it instead of guessing which side a borderline set belongs on.

Answer with a single JSON object:
{"operation": "move_to_x" | "move_to_y" | "new_block" | "none", "predicate": [{"field": "<field name>", "operator": "equals" | "contains" | "not_contains" | "regex" | "is_null" | "is_not_null" | "gte" | "lte" | "between", "value": "<text>"}], "reason": "<one sentence>"}
"gte" and "lte" compare the first number found in the field against the value; "between" takes "value": [low, high] and is true when the number falls in that range, endpoints included.
You may give several rules at once, up to five, as {"actions": [{...}, {...}], "reason": "<one sentence>"}; they are applied in the order written, and each one acts on the grouping the previous rules left behind.
"move_to_x" moves every record of group Y that satisfies the condition into group X; "move_to_y" is the mirror. "new_block" takes the records of either group that satisfy the condition into a group of their own — use this instead of move_to_x/move_to_y when you want to isolate a suspect subset without committing it to either existing group. "none" leaves the grouping unchanged and needs no condition. Conditions within one rule are combined with and.

Iteration 114: Selected program 3 score: 0.31276720239071254
Iteration 114: Proposed new text for message: Two groups of records, group X and group Y, are shown below. One pair of records, one from each group, is known to describe the same real-world entity, yet the two records were placed in different groups.

{STATS}

Known same-entity pair:
{SEED}

Group X sample:
{SAMPLE_X}

Group Y sample:
{SAMPLE_Y}

Your task: write one small edit that puts the two seed records into the same group, without dragging along records that do not belong to that entity. Work through these steps in order.

Step 1 — pick the direction. Compare the two true group sizes given above. The source group is the LARGER one; you will move records out of it into the smaller one. If group Y is larger, the operation is "move_to_x". If group X is larger, the operation is "move_to_y". Moving a few records out of a large group into a small one is cheap and can even reduce comparisons; moving records into a large group is expensive.

Step 2 — build the condition from the seed record that sits in the source group. Copy the longest distinctive phrase you can from its most specific field — most of a title or name, or an exact model number. Copy it character for character from the record text shown above. Do not retype it from memory, do not fix spelling, do not complete a truncated phrase: if the value does not literally appear in the record, the condition matches nothing and the edit is wasted. Prefer the "contains" operator over "equals", since "equals" fails on any extra punctuation or prefix.

Step 3 — test the condition against the source group's sample. Go through the sample records of the source group one by one and count how many your condition catches. It must catch the seed record. If it also catches records that are clearly about different entities, or if it catches more than a handful of the sample, narrow it by adding a second condition on another specific field — or give up and answer "none". Remember the sample is a small fraction: whatever share of the sample you catch, the condition will catch the same share of the whole group.

Step 4 — final safety checks:
- Never use a broad field (venue, manufacturer, category, year) as the only condition. Many unrelated records share those values, and moving a large slice of one group into the other costs far more in comparisons than one repaired pair is worth.
- If the two seed records give you no phrase that is both present in the source record and specific to this one entity, answer "none". Doing nothing scores better than a broad guess.

Answer with a single JSON object:
{"operation": "move_to_x" | "move_to_y" | "new_block" | "none", "predicate": [{"field": "<field name>", "operator": "equals" | "contains" | "not_contains" | "regex" | "is_null" | "is_not_null" | "gte" | "lte" | "between", "value": "<text>"}], "reason": "<one sentence>"}
"gte" and "lte" compare the first number found in the field against the value; "between" takes "value": [low, high] and is true when the number falls in that range, endpoints included.
You may give several rules at once, up to five, as {"actions": [{...}, {...}], "reason": "<one sentence>"}; they are applied in the order written, and each one acts on the grouping the previous rules left behind. A single rule is almost always enough here.
"move_to_x" moves every record of group Y that satisfies the condition into group X; "move_to_y" is the mirror. "new_block" takes the records of either group that satisfy the condition into a group of their own — use it only to isolate a suspect subset without committing it to either existing group. "none" leaves the grouping unchanged and needs no condition. Conditions within one rule are combined with and.

Iteration 115: Proposed new text for message: Two groups of records, group X and group Y, are shown below. One record in group X and one record in group Y are known to describe the same real-world entity (the "seed pair"), but the grouping split them apart. Your job is to write one rule that brings that entity's records into one group — without dragging along records that describe other entities. The rule is a condition on record fields, and it is applied to EVERY record of the source group, including the many records not shown here.

Group sizes (true size of each group, and how many of its records are shown below):
{STATS}

Known same-entity pair, one record from each group:
{SEED}

Sample of group X:
{SAMPLE_X}

Sample of group Y:
{SAMPLE_Y}

Follow these steps in order.

Step 1. Read the two seed records. Find the longest run of words that appears in BOTH records (ignore upper/lower case differences while comparing). This is usually most of a title, a product name, or a model number. Cut off any part where the two records differ in spelling, hyphens, spacing, or digits; keep only the part that is identical in both.

Step 2. If that identical run is shorter than 4 words and is not a model number or product code, answer none. A short or common value — a venue, a manufacturer, a publisher, a year, a single word — matches many unrelated records and does damage.

Step 3. Choose the direction using the true group sizes given above:
- If group X is bigger than group Y, use "move_to_y" (this moves matching records OUT of group X into group Y).
- If group Y is bigger than group X, use "move_to_x" (this moves matching records OUT of group Y into group X).
Moving records out of the bigger group into the smaller one is the cheap direction.

Step 4. The group that records move OUT of is the source group. Verify your fragment against it:
- Copy the fragment character-for-character from the seed record that sits in the source group — same upper/lower case, same punctuation. Do not retype it from memory and do not fix its spelling; a value not copied literally matches zero records.
- Look through the source group's sample for other records containing the fragment. Records that repeat the same title or product name are the same entity — matching them is good. But if any record containing the fragment clearly describes a DIFFERENT entity, the fragment is too broad: answer none.

Step 5. Answer with ONE JSON object and nothing else:
{"operation": "move_to_x" | "move_to_y" | "none", "predicate": [{"field": "<field the fragment appears in>", "operator": "contains", "value": "<the exact copied fragment>"}], "reason": "<one sentence>"}
For "none", use an empty predicate list: {"operation": "none", "predicate": [], "reason": "<one sentence>"}

Hard rules:
- Exactly one condition. Never add a second condition on year, venue, pages, or publisher — their formatting differs from record to record, and the combined rule then matches nothing.
- Always use the operator "contains", never "equals" — "equals" fails on any extra punctuation or trailing text.
- Never build a condition on venue, publisher, manufacturer, or year alone. These are shared by many unrelated records.
- The value must appear literally in the text of a record shown above. If you cannot find a value that is both specific to the seed entity and literally present, answer none. Answering none is better than guessing.

Iteration 115: Selected program 3 score: 0.31276720239071254
Iteration 115: Proposed new text for message: Two groups of records — group X and group Y — were produced by a grouping procedure. One record from X and one from Y describe the same real-world entity but ended up split. Decide one change to the grouping that would reunite records like these, stated as a condition on fields (not as a list of the records shown).

{STATS}

Known same-entity pair (one record from each group):
{SEED}

Sample of records currently in group X:
{SAMPLE_X}

Sample of records currently in group Y:
{SAMPLE_Y}

Operations:
- "move_to_x": move every record of Y that satisfies the condition into X.
- "move_to_y": mirror — move every record of X that satisfies the condition into Y.
- "new_block": pull the records of either group satisfying the condition out into a new group of their own. Use this when you want to isolate a suspect subset rather than commit it to X or Y.
- "none": do nothing. No condition needed.

Which label is X and which is Y carries no meaning — the two are ordered by an internal id.

How the count of within-group comparisons responds (this is what the score penalises):
- Moving k records from a group of size a into a group of size b changes comparisons by k*(b - a + k). Negative when k < a - b, i.e. moving a modest slice out of a much larger group into a smaller one both reunites records and lowers cost.
- Moving a big slice of a large group into another large group is expensive and rarely pays.
- The sample shown is a fixed uniform fraction of the group, so the number of sample rows a condition would match scales up to the true group.

Writing the condition:
- The value must appear literally in the text of records you intend to match. Check the samples above, not just the seed pair — a value invented from memory of similar records matches 0 records here.
- Prefer a long distinctive phrase (most of a title, a model number, a full product name) over a short generic token. Short/generic values (a common venue, manufacturer, single word, bare year) sweep in a large slice of unrelated records — the extra comparisons cost more than the reunited pair is worth.
- If the field that ties the seed pair together is broad (venue, manufacturer, year), do not use it alone. Either narrow it with a second, more specific field whose value also appears literally in both sample records, or answer "none".
- Before writing a rule that would match a large fraction of a big group into a small one, or unite two large chunks, run the k*(b - a + k) arithmetic. If it comes out positive and large, prefer "none".
- If no value in the sample is both specific to the shared entity and unlikely to sweep in unrelated records, answer "none" — do not guess.

Iteration 116: Proposed new text for message: Two groups of records, group X and group Y, come out of a grouping procedure. One record from X and one from Y are known to describe the same real-world entity, but the procedure split them apart. Your job is to name a condition on record fields such that applying it to every record of a group (not only the ones shown) would reunite records like these without dragging in many that do not belong together.

{STATS}

Known same-entity pair (one record from each group):
{SEED}

Sample of records currently in group X:
{SAMPLE_X}

Sample of records currently in group Y:
{SAMPLE_Y}

How to think about the choice:
- The two records in the known pair share some content. Find a phrase, identifier, or value they both carry that is specific enough to pick out the same entity and unlikely to appear in unrelated records. A distinctive phrase from a title or name field usually beats a broad category value shared by many records.
- A predicate that matches a large fraction of the other group will move many records at once. If most of those records do not belong with the group they land in, the number of within-group comparisons grows sharply and the score is penalised at five times the relative growth. Prefer a condition that matches a small, focused subset containing the shown pair.
- If the two records look nearly identical apart from typography or word order, mirror the exact shared substring rather than a paraphrase.
- If nothing in the shown records is specific enough to name without hitting unrelated records, choose "none".

Operations available:
- "move_to_x": every record of Y satisfying the condition moves into X.
- "move_to_y": every record of X satisfying the condition moves into Y.
- "new_block": the records of either group satisfying the condition are pulled out into a group of their own. Use this when the matching subset looks coherent on its own but you are unsure it belongs with either X or Y as they stand.
- "none": leave the grouping alone; no condition needed.

Iteration 117: Proposed new text for message: Two groups of records, group X and group Y, come out of a grouping procedure. One record from X and one from Y are known to describe the same real-world entity, but the procedure split them apart. Your job is to name a condition on record fields such that applying it to every record of a group (not only the ones shown) would reunite records like these without dragging in many that do not belong together.

{STATS}

Known same-entity pair (one record from each group):
{SEED}

Sample of records currently in group X:
{SAMPLE_X}

Sample of records currently in group Y:
{SAMPLE_Y}

How to pick the rule:

1. Read the two records in the known pair side by side. Find the longest stretch of text they both carry word-for-word — usually a title, a product name, or a distinctive phrase from one. Copy that stretch verbatim, matching the case and punctuation of one of the two records. Do not paraphrase, translate, abbreviate or normalise it. If the two records write it differently, use the shorter form that appears in both.

2. Prefer the "title" or "name" field over "venue", "manufacturer", "year", "authors" or other category-like fields. Broad category values (a conference name, a publisher, a common brand) match many records that have nothing else in common; every extra record moved inflates within-group comparisons and the score is charged five times the relative growth.

3. Check the field name and the value against the records shown. If the field does not appear in these records, or the value cannot be seen in the field of at least one shown record, the rule will match nothing. In that case pick another field or another wording.

4. Look at the group sizes in {STATS}. Moving records into a group already many times larger than the source is expensive per record. A narrow rule that catches only the wanted handful pays little; a rule that catches many records of the larger group into the smaller one is fine only if you are confident every one of them belongs.

5. If the two shown records share no phrase distinctive enough to name without pulling in unrelated records, answer "none". A cheap null move beats a bad edit.

Operations:
- "move_to_x": every record of Y satisfying the rule moves into X.
- "move_to_y": every record of X satisfying the rule moves into Y.
- "new_block": records of either group satisfying the rule are pulled into a group of their own. Use when the matching set looks like its own entity, distinct from both X and Y.
- "none": leave the grouping alone; no rule needed.

Iteration 118: Selected program 4 score: 0.4240247233636702
Iteration 118: Proposed new text for message: Two groups of records, group X and group Y, came out of a grouping procedure that split apart one pair known to describe the same real-world entity. Your task: state a condition on record fields such that every record of a group satisfying it (not only the ones shown) would reunite pairs like this one, while dragging in as few unrelated records as possible.

{STATS}

Known same-entity pair (one from each group):
{SEED}

Sample from group X:
{SAMPLE_X}

Sample from group Y:
{SAMPLE_Y}

How the score works, kept short:
- You earn credit each time your condition reunites separated same-entity records anywhere in the collection, not only in the pair shown.
- You pay five times the relative growth in within-group comparisons across the whole collection. A rule that sweeps in dozens of unrelated records nearly always loses more than it gains.
- Group sizes matter: moving k records from a bigger group into a smaller one changes comparisons by k*(smaller_size - bigger_size + k). Moving a handful out of a much larger group into a smaller one can reunite and cut comparisons at the same time. Moving many out of the smaller group into the bigger one raises comparisons fast.

How to pick the rule:
1. Read the two records of the known pair side by side. Find the longest word-for-word overlap they share — usually a chunk of a title, product name, or another distinctive text field. Copy that chunk verbatim from one of the two, keeping its case (the "contains" operator is case-insensitive, so exact case is not required, but keep the words in the order they appear).
2. Prefer one narrow rule that keys off that verbatim chunk in a text field. Avoid values that name a category, a venue, a conference series, a publisher, a manufacturer alone, or a bare author surname — those hit far too many rows.
3. Check roughly how many records in each shown sample would match your rule. If it looks like more than a small handful, shorten it or add a second condition on another field with AND to tighten it. If nothing in the two records is specific enough to name without hitting unrelated records, answer "none".
4. Direction: if X is much larger than Y in {STATS} and the shared content clearly belongs with X-style records, "move_to_x" (moving Y records into X) tends to hurt comparisons — flip to "move_to_y" instead. When one group is much larger, prefer moving the small matching set out of the large group.
5. Use "new_block" only when the matching subset looks like its own coherent thing that fits neither X nor Y as they stand.

Operations:
- "move_to_x": every record of Y satisfying the condition moves into X.
- "move_to_y": every record of X satisfying the condition moves into Y.
- "new_block": records of either group satisfying the condition become a group of their own.
- "none": leave the grouping alone; no condition needed.

Operators: "equals", "contains" (case-insensitive text match), "not_contains", "regex" (full regular expression on the field), "is_null", "is_not_null", "gte" and "lte" (compare the first number found in the field to "value"), "between" (takes "value": [low, high], endpoints included). Multiple entries in one "predicate" list are combined with AND. Field names must be ones that actually appear in the records shown.

Reply with one JSON object. Either one rule:
{"operation": "move_to_x" | "move_to_y" | "new_block" | "none",
 "predicate": [{"field": "<field name>", "operator": "<op>", "value": "<text>"}],
 "reason": "<one sentence>"}

 "reason": "<one sentence>"}

Iteration 120: Proposed new text for message: Two groups from a grouping procedure, X and Y, are shown below with their true sizes and how many records of each are shown. One pair known to describe the same real-world entity landed on opposite sides. Name a change to the grouping — as a condition on record fields — that would reunite pairs like this one without pulling in many records that do not belong.

{STATS}

Known same-entity pair (one from each group):
{SEED}

Sample from group X:
{SAMPLE_X}

Sample from group Y:
{SAMPLE_Y}

Method:

1. Put the two seed records side by side. Find a stretch of text that appears in both records word-for-word — a full title, a product name, a model code, a paper name. Copy it exactly as it appears in one of the two, keeping the case as-is. "contains" matches case-insensitively already, so no need to lowercase.

2. Do not use short generic tokens on their own: a lone manufacturer name, a lone venue name, a lone author surname, a single common word, or a year sweeps in many unrelated records and rarely pays back.

3. Read the phrase you copied against the other group's sample. If several records there carry the same phrase but are clearly different entities (a shared series name, a co-authored paper's title reused on other papers, a product line shared across items), add a second field condition inside the same rule. The two conditions are combined with AND. Only add a second condition if the field you name is present in both seed records — a condition on a field that is absent from the seed pair will usually match zero.

4. Prefer "contains" over "equals". "equals" needs the whole field value identical including trailing punctuation and case, and often matches nothing when the two representations differ by a period or a minor formatting change.

5. Choose the direction by the group sizes shown in {STATS}. Moving a few records out of a much larger group into a smaller one both reunites the pair and lowers the total number of within-group comparisons. If the entity clearly belongs with the smaller group, pick "move_to_y" over "move_to_x" (or the reverse). The labels X and Y are arbitrary — internal id order, no meaning.

6. "new_block" pulls records satisfying the condition, from either group, into a group of their own. Use it when the matching subset looks like one coherent entity but neither X nor Y as they stand is a clean home for it.

7. If nothing in the two seed records is specific enough to write a condition that would not also match unrelated records, answer "none". A no-op costs zero; a broad guess usually costs more than it earns.

Scoring, in one line: for every separated same-entity pair your condition reunites anywhere in the collection you gain credit; you pay five times the relative growth in within-group comparisons. A rule matching one to a few records is almost always safe. A rule matching twenty or more records has to reunite same-entity records in the great majority of cases, or it loses more than it earns.

Reply with one JSON object. One rule:
{"operation": "move_to_x" | "move_to_y" | "new_block" | "none",
 "predicate": [{"field": "<field name>", "operator": "equals" | "contains" | "not_contains" | "regex" | "is_null" | "is_not_null" | "gte" | "lte" | "between", "value": "<text>"}],
 "reason": "<one sentence>"}

Or up to five rules at once, applied in order, each acting on the grouping the earlier rules left:
{"actions": [{"operation": "...", "predicate": [...]}, {"operation": "...", "predicate": [...]}],
 "reason": "<one sentence>"}

Operator notes: "contains" is a case-insensitive text match on the field; "regex" is a full regular expression on the field; "gte" and "lte" compare the first number found in the field to "value"; "between" takes "value": [low, high] and is true when the number lies in the range, endpoints included. Conditions within one rule are combined with AND. Field names must be ones that actually appear in the records shown.

Iteration 122: Proposed new text for message: Group X and group Y come from a grouping procedure that put one known same-entity pair on opposite sides. Name one condition on record fields; the grouper applies it to every record of the target group, not just the ones shown here, and pulls the matches across.

{STATS}

Seed pair (one record from group X, one from group Y):
{SEED}

Records currently in group X (a uniform sample):
{SAMPLE_X}

Records currently in group Y (a uniform sample):
{SAMPLE_Y}

What the scorer rewards, in one line: same-entity pairs reunited across the whole collection, less five times the relative growth in within-group comparisons. So a rule matching one to a handful of records is almost always safe; a rule matching twenty or more only pays off if nearly all of them are the same entity as the seed.

Recipe that has worked well in past rounds:

1. Line up the two seed records. Locate the longest run of characters they share word-for-word — commonly a title, a product name, a paper name, or a model code. Copy that run verbatim from one of the two, exactly as written.

2. Prefer a distinctive multi-word phrase from a title-like or name-like field over a short token, a category, a venue, a publisher, or a year. Broad category values ("VLDB", "SIGMOD Conference", "International Conference on Management of Data", "Very Large Data Bases", "unpublished manuscript") match dozens of records at once and drag apart pairs that were already grouped correctly.

3. Do not use is_null / is_not_null on its own, and do not filter on year alone — both match too broadly to earn back the cost.

4. Direction: the seed record living in group Y satisfies your condition → operation is move_to_x (the other group takes it in). Mirror the other way for move_to_y. If you cannot find a phrase that would pick out only the seed's twins and would not also pick out unrelated records, answer "none" rather than guess — a wrong sweep costs more than doing nothing.

5. new_block is the right answer when the phrase you found is present in both groups and picks out a small consistent subset in each — pull those into their own group instead of committing to X or Y.

Output format. A single JSON object; the reader accepts either shape:

Single rule:
{"operation": "move_to_x" | "move_to_y" | "new_block" | "none",
 "predicate": [{"field": "<field name>", "operator": "equals" | "contains" | "not_contains" | "regex" | "is_null" | "is_not_null" | "gte" | "lte" | "between", "value": "<text>"}],
 "reason": "<one sentence>"}

Up to five rules applied in order, each acting on the grouping the previous ones left behind:
{"actions": [{...}, {...}, ...], "reason": "<one sentence>"}

Operator notes: predicate entries inside one rule combine with and; gte / lte compare against the first number found in the field; between takes "value": [low, high] with endpoints included; "none" needs no predicate.

Iteration 123: Selected program 4 score: 0.4240247233636702
Iteration 123: Proposed new text for message: Group X and group Y came from a grouping procedure. One pair of records, one from each group, is known to describe the same real-world entity but was split between the two. Name one change to the grouping — a condition on record fields — that would reunite pairs like this one across the whole collection, applied to every record of the target group and not only the ones shown.

{STATS}

Known same-entity pair (one from X, one from Y):
{SEED}

A uniform sample from group X:
{SAMPLE_X}

A uniform sample from group Y:
{SAMPLE_Y}

What the scorer pays for, in one line: separated same-entity pairs your rule reunites anywhere in the collection, less five times the relative growth in within-group comparisons. So a rule that matches one to a handful of records is nearly always safe; a rule matching twenty or more only pays off when almost all of them are the same entity as the seed pair.

How to pick the rule:

1. Set the two seed records beside each other. Find the longest stretch of text that appears in both, word-for-word — a full paper title, a product name, a model code, or a distinctive phrase from a title or name field. Copy that stretch verbatim from one of the two records, keeping its case and punctuation as they appear. The "contains" operator ignores case, so no rewriting is needed.

2. Do not use short generic tokens on their own — a lone brand, a category word, a year, a common venue. Those match many unrelated records. Prefer a phrase of several distinctive words together, or an identifier (SKU, model number, ISBN-like code) that is unique to one entity.

3. Choose the operation by looking at the field:
   - If the shared phrase is in a title, name, or product-name field, use a single condition with operator "contains" on that field and the phrase as the value.
   - If the shared phrase is a code that should match exactly, use operator "equals" on the code field.
   - Combine two conditions with AND only when a single one would sweep in too many unrelated records; each extra condition also shrinks how many true same-entity records the rule catches.

4. Choose the direction. "move_to_x" moves every Y record satisfying the condition into X. "move_to_y" is the mirror. The labels X and Y carry no meaning by themselves — the arithmetic of comparisons depends on group sizes, which {STATS} reports: moving records into a much smaller group grows the comparison count less than moving them into a much larger one. When the two records look alike but you are unsure either current group is the right home, use "new_block": the matches are pulled out into a group of their own.

5. If nothing in the seed pair is specific enough to name without hitting unrelated records, return "none" with an empty condition list.

Answer with one JSON object. Two shapes are accepted.

One rule:
{"operation": "move_to_x" | "move_to_y" | "new_block" | "none",
 "predicate": [{"field": "<field name>", "operator": "equals" | "contains" | "not_contains" | "regex" | "is_null" | "is_not_null" | "gte" | "lte" | "between", "value": "<text>"}],
 "reason": "<one sentence>"}

Up to five rules at once, applied in order, each acting on the grouping the previous ones left:
{"actions": [{"operation": "...", "predicate": [...]}, ...],
 "reason": "<one sentence>"}

Notes on operators: "contains" is a case-insensitive text match on the field. "regex" is a full regular expression on the field. "gte" and "lte" compare the first number found in the field to "value". "between" takes "value": [low, high] and is true when the number lies in the range, endpoints included. Conditions inside one rule are combined with AND. Field names must be ones that actually appear in the records shown.

Iteration 124: Proposed new text for message: Two groups from a grouping procedure, X and Y, are shown below with their true sizes and how many records of each are shown. One pair known to describe the same real-world entity landed on opposite sides. Name a change to the grouping — as a condition on record fields — that would reunite pairs like this one without pulling in many records that do not belong.

{STATS}

Known same-entity pair (one from each group):
{SEED}

Sample from group X:
{SAMPLE_X}

Sample from group Y:
{SAMPLE_Y}

The condition you write is applied to every record of a group, including the great majority you were not shown. So the sample is your only window onto what else the rule will sweep in. Read it before you commit.

Steps:

1. Line the two seed records up field by field. Find one field present in both where a run of characters appears verbatim — a full title, a full product or paper name, a model code, a distinctive catalogue number. Copy that run from one of the two seed records, keeping case and punctuation as-is. "contains" already ignores case.

2. Before you finalise, scan the sample from the group you would be moving records out of for that exact run. Count roughly how many records there carry it. One or two matches: the rule is cheap and almost certainly safe. Five to twenty: the sample suggests the same-entity share must be high for the rule to break even. More than twenty in the sample: the true match count is a large multiple of that — only justified if the phrase itself names one specific entity and every match will be it.

3. If the run you found is a common series name, a product line reused across items, a paper title reused across variants, or a venue or author name that appears on many unrelated records, add a second condition on a different field to narrow it down. Both conditions must be on fields that are actually present in the seed pair (a condition on a field the seed lacks usually matches zero). Conditions inside one rule are combined with AND.

4. Prefer "contains" over "equals". "equals" needs the whole field byte-identical including trailing punctuation and will often match nothing when the two sides differ by a period, a space, or a capital.

5. Pick the direction from the sizes in {STATS}. If the entity clearly belongs with the smaller of the two groups, move into it: taking a few records out of a much larger group into a smaller one both reunites the pair and lowers the total number of within-group comparisons. The labels X and Y themselves carry no meaning — they are ordered by internal id.

6. "new_block" pulls matching records from either group into a fresh group of their own. Use it when the matching subset looks like one coherent entity but neither X nor Y as they stand is a clean home for it — for example, when both current groups are dominated by a different entity and only a handful on each side belong with the seed.

7. If nothing shared between the two seed records is specific enough to write a rule that would not also match a crowd of unrelated records, answer "none". A no-op costs zero; a broad guess usually costs more than it earns.

You may write one rule:
{"operation": "move_to_x" | "move_to_y" | "new_block" | "none",
 "predicate": [{"field": "<field name>", "operator": "equals" | "contains" | "not_contains" | "regex" | "is_null" | "is_not_null" | "gte" | "lte" | "between", "value": "<text>"}],
 "reason": "<one sentence>"}

Or up to five rules, applied in order, each acting on the grouping the earlier rules left:
{"actions": [{"operation": "...", "predicate": [...]}, {"operation": "...", "predicate": [...]}],
 "reason": "<one sentence>"}

When several rules make sense, chain them: for instance, first pull the tightly named subset into place with a long verbatim phrase, then a second, narrower rule for a differently-worded variant of the same entity. Each rule sees the grouping the previous ones left, so a broad rule placed after a narrow one only touches what remains.

Operator notes: "contains" is a case-insensitive text match; "regex" is a full regular expression on the field; "gte" and "lte" compare the first number found in the field to "value"; "between" takes "value": [low, high] and is true when the number lies in the range, endpoints included. Field names must be ones that actually appear in the records shown. Conditions within a single rule are combined with AND.

Scoring, one line: every separated same-entity pair your condition reunites anywhere in the collection earns credit; you pay five times the relative growth in within-group comparisons. Reuniting roughly a tenth of the separated pairs shown is worth about the same as a two-percent growth in comparisons costs. A rule matching one to a few records is nearly always safe; a rule matching twenty or more only pays off when the great majority of those matches are the same entity as the seed.

{STATS}

Known same-entity pair (one from each group):
{SEED}

Sample from group X:

Work through these in order:

1) Which field, present in both seed records, holds a long distinctive phrase that appears verbatim across the two — a full title, a full product name, a paper name, a catalogue or model code? Pick that field. If no field of the seed pair carries such a phrase, jump to step 6.

2) Copy the longest verbatim run of that field from one of the two seed records. Keep the case as written; "contains" ignores case anyway. Do not use short generic tokens on their own — a bare brand, a bare venue, a bare author surname, a single common word, a bare year. Those match many unrelated records and rarely pay back.

3) Read the same field across the other group's sample. If several records there carry the same phrase but are plainly different entities (a shared series name, a shared product line, a title recycled by a different paper), add a second condition on another field that IS present in both seed records. The two conditions are combined with AND. Never add a condition on a field missing from one of the seed records — it will match zero.

4) Prefer "contains" over "equals". "equals" needs the full field value identical down to trailing punctuation and case, and often matches nothing.

5) Pick a direction from {STATS}. Moving a small number of records out of the larger group into the smaller one both reunites the seed pair and lowers the total number of within-group comparisons. Move the matching records into whichever of X or Y the entity clearly belongs with; the labels themselves are arbitrary (internal id order, no meaning). 6) If no field of the seed pair holds anything specific enough to write a condition that would not also sweep in unrelated records, answer "none". A no-op costs zero; a broad guess usually costs more than it earns.

Scoring, in one line: for every separated same-entity pair your condition reunites anywhere in the collection you gain credit; you pay five times the relative growth in within-group comparisons. A rule matching one to a few records is almost always safe. A rule matching twenty or more has to reunite same-entity records in the great majority of cases, or it loses more than it earns.

Iteration 126: Selected program 0 score: 0.17391488844243724
Iteration 126: Proposed new text for message: Two groups, X and Y, came from a grouping procedure that put one known same-entity pair on opposite sides. Name one condition on record fields — the grouper applies it to every record of the source group, not only the ones shown — that puts pairs like this one back together without pulling in records that do not belong.

{STATS}

Known same-entity pair (one record from X, one from Y):
{SEED}

Sample of group X:
{SAMPLE_X}

Sample of group Y:
{SAMPLE_Y}

Two things drive the score. Reuniting a separated same-entity pair anywhere in the collection earns credit; every extra within-group comparison your rule creates costs, at a rate of five times the collection-wide relative growth. A rule that matches one to a few records is almost always safe. A rule that matches twenty or more only pays off when nearly every match is the same entity as the seed — bare category-level fields (a venue name, a manufacturer name, a year) almost never clear that bar.

Direction matters too. Moving k records from a group of size a into a group of size b changes comparisons by k*(b - a + k). If a is much larger than b, that number is negative: you reunite records and shrink comparisons at once. So when the two group sizes in {STATS} differ, prefer moving out of the larger one. The label X or Y carries no meaning on its own — read the sizes.

Write the condition like this:

1) Look at the two seed records side by side. Find one field present in both that carries a long, distinctive run of characters word-for-word across the two — a full product name, a full paper title, a full model or catalogue code. That verbatim run is your value.

2) Copy it exactly from one of the two seed records; keep the case you see. The "contains" operator ignores case, so no need to lowercase. Do not shorten to a bare brand, a bare surname, a bare venue — those match far too much.

3) Glance at the sample of the group you would move records out of. If the run you picked also appears in records of that sample that are plainly unrelated to the seed pair, tighten the rule: add a second condition on another field (an "and" inside the same rule), or pick a longer, rarer run of text.

4) If no field of the seed pair carries a shared distinctive run — the two records agree only on generic fragments — answer with "none". A rule you are not confident in loses more than it saves.

Reply with a single JSON object:

{"operation": "move_to_x" | "move_to_y" | "new_block" | "none",
 "predicate": [{"field": "<name>", "operator": "equals" | "contains" | "not_contains" | "regex" | "is_null" | "is_not_null" | "gte" | "lte" | "between", "value": "<text>"}],
 "reason": "<one sentence>"}

"move_to_x" moves every record of group Y satisfying the condition into X; "move_to_y" is the mirror; "new_block" pulls the records of either group satisfying it into a group of their own; "none" leaves the grouping alone and needs no predicate. Conditions inside one rule combine with and. "gte"/"lte" compare the first number found in the field against the value; "between" takes "value": [low, high] and is true when the number falls in that range, endpoints included.

If a single condition would either miss the seed pair or drag in too many strangers, you may send several rules at once, applied in order, each acting on what the previous ones left behind:

{"actions": [ {"operation": ..., "predicate": [...]}, ... up to five ... ],
 "reason": "<one sentence>"}
